Fourier–Stieltjes category for twisted groupoid actions
Alcides Buss, Bartosz Kwaśniewski, Andrew McKee, Adam Skalski

Abstract
. We extend the theory of Fourier–Stieltjes algebras to the category of twisted actions by étale groupoids on arbitrary C ∗ \mathrm{C}^{*} -bundles, generalising theories constructed previously by Bédos and Conti for twisted group actions on unital C ∗ \mathrm{C}^{*} -algebras, and by Renault and others for groupoid C ∗ \mathrm{C}^{*} -algebras, in each case motivated by the classical theory of Fourier–Stieltjes algebras of discrete groups. To this end we develop a toolbox including, among other things, a theory of multiplier C ∗ \mathrm{C}^{*} -correspondences, multiplier C ∗ \mathrm{C}^{*} -correspondence bundles, Busby–Smith twisted groupoid actions, and the associated crossed products, equivariant representations and Fell’s absorption theorems. For a fixed étale groupoid 𝒢 \mathcal{G} a Fourier–Stieltjes multiplier is a family of maps acting on fibres, arising from an equivariant representation. It corresponds to a certain fibre-preserving strict completely bounded map between twisted full (or reduced) crossed products. We establish a KSGNS-type dilation result which shows that the correspondence above restricts to a bijection between positive-definite multipliers and a particular class of completely positive maps. Further, we introduce a subclass of Fourier multipliers, that enjoys a natural absorption property with respect to Fourier–Stieltjes multipliers and gives rise to ‘reduced to full’ multiplier maps on crossed products. Finally, we provide several applications of the theory developed, for example to the approximation properties, such as weak containment or nuclearity, of the crossed products and actions in question, and discuss outstanding open problems.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 46L05; Secondary 20E26, 22A22, 43A22, 46L55

Introduction

The notions of Fourier–Stieltjes and Fourier algebras of general locally compact groups, motivated by the concepts appearing earlier in classical, abelian harmonic analysis, were first introduced sixty years ago in the thesis of Eymard
[
30
]
.
Recall that the
Fourier–Stieltjes algebra

B(\Gamma)
of a given discrete group
\Gamma
can be viewed in at least three different guises:

(1)

as the algebra of functions given by coefficients of unitary representations of
\Gamma
, with the pointwise multiplication;

(2)

as the dual of the universal group
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra
\mathrm{C}^{*}(\Gamma)
, equipped with the ‘convolution’ type product induced by the comultiplication of
\mathrm{C}^{*}(\Gamma)
;

(3)

as the span of completely positive (in short cp) Herz–Schur multipliers of
\mathrm{C}^{*}(\Gamma)
, with the multiplication given as the composition of maps.

Similarly, the
Fourier algebra

A(\Gamma)
, which is an ideal in
B(\Gamma)
, can be seen as the algebra of coefficients of the left regular representation, as the predual of the group von Neumann algebra
\mathrm{vN}(\Gamma)
, or as a certain subclass of Herz–Schur multipliers of
\mathrm{C}^{*}(\Gamma)
(or of
\mathrm{vN}(\Gamma)
).
Note that developing the equivalence between the first and last points of view displayed above requires proving a version of a dilation result for positive-definite functions, which in this context is nothing but the GNS construction.
On the other hand, the fact that
A(\Gamma)
is an ideal inside
B(\Gamma)
is a consequence of Fell’s absorption principle: the tensor product of a regular representation and an arbitrary representation is unitarily equivalent to a multiple of the regular representation.

This flexibility is one of the reasons why the aforementioned Banach algebras have played a significant role in the study of operator algebras related to groups and in the noncommutative harmonic analysis in general (we refer to the recent book
[
37
]
for an introduction to the topic and several applications).
It is thus natural that together with the development of various generalisations of group operator algebras the literature has seen several attempts to investigate analogues of Fourier–Stieltjes algebras in these new contexts.
We would like to mention here for example the study of Fourier–Stieltjes algebras of locally compact quantum groups à la Kustermans–Vaes
[
41
]
, as discussed for example in
[
21
]
.
The study of Fourier algebras, Fourier–Stieltjes algebras and related multiplier maps for measure groupoids was initiated in the articles
[
59
,
55
,
50
]
, with the main focus on trying to provide the equivalence between the first and the third point of view offered above; in particular on proving suitable versions of dilation and absorption results.
The two specific cases which are most relevant for our paper are however the ones encoding various types of non-commutative topological dynamics, i.e. that of the étale groupoid
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebras and that of the
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebraic twisted crossed products, which we will discuss next.

Locally compact Hausdorff étale grupoids were introduced to the
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebraic world in the early 1980s in
[
56
]
, largely motivated by the developments related to equivalence relations and von Neumann algebra theory, that later culminated in the celebrated characterisation of Cartan inclusions
[
60
]
.
An important special case of the étale groupoid
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra construction is that of the crossed product
C(X)\rtimes\Gamma
, where
X
is a compact space equipped with an action of a discrete group
\Gamma
.
It has however taken relatively long until the theory of Fourier–Stieltjes algebras and related multiplier maps was developed in the context of general
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebraic crossed products (although
[
54
, Section 7.6]
provides a description of the state space of the crossed product
A\rtimes\Gamma
, and an attempt to introduce the algebraic structure on this space was undertaken in
[
33
]
).
During the last few years we have seen a flow of work, notably by Bédos and Conti, see
[
8
,
9
]
and references therein, which studied ‘multiplier type’ maps on crossed products by twisted actions of discrete groups on
unital

\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebras.
In particular, in the articles
[
7
,
8
]
both the dilation, for ‘kernels of positive type’, and the absorption results for the so-called ‘induced representations’ were established.
The new context necessitated the use of Hilbert modules and
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondences, similarly to the quantum group framework mentioned earlier, as seen for example in
[
22
]
.

The articles
[
24
]
and
[
47
]
use multiplier maps similar to those considered by Bédos and Conti to study approximation properties of the crossed products in question.
We should mention here that the key technique which makes the last application possible, and goes back to the context of group
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebras, is the so-called
Haagerup trick
, which provides a way to ‘average’ arbitrary bounded linear maps on the crossed product algebra into multipliers.
In the context of twisted groupoid actions a version of the Haagerup trick was applied very recently in
[
42
]
by two of the authors of this work, to study the relative Haagerup property of
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebras in connection with the UCT property.
This was possibly the first time multiplier type maps appeared for the groupoid crossed products.

\text{Proposition \ref{prop:Haagerup_trick}}\end{array}\right)
\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\text{{nuclearity} criteria}\\
\text{and characterisation}\\

Figure 1.
Structure and relationship between developed steps/results

In this paper, inspired by the developments mentioned above, we undertake a systematic study of Fourier–Stieltjes and Fourier multipliers for crossed products by twisted actions of étale groupoids on arbitrary
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-bundles.
To include the work of Bédos and Conti
[
6
,
7
,
8
]
as a special case, we consider here twisted groupoid actions á la Busby–Smith
[
12
]
with twists taking values in multiplier bundles.
Such actions appear in
[
17
]
but their crossed products, apart from the group case, have not yet been studied.
In particular, they are different from the Green–Renault twisted groupoid actions considered in
[
57
,
58
]
, where the twist is abelian (usually scalar valued).
It has also occurred to us that, instead of looking at Banach algebras of multipliers for a single action, it is more natural and useful to study
Banach categories of multipliers
between different actions of the same groupoid.
The main steps in our development, as well as the relationships between them, are schematically presented in Figure
1
.

Apart from the novelties mentioned above we also for the first time consider crossed product multipliers for actions on
non-unital

C^{*}
-algebras.
To do so we develop systematically a theory of
multiplier
C^{*}
-correspondences
.
It seems that so far this notion was considered only in the special case of Hilbert bimodules
[
26
,
62
]
, and we believe that the results of this investigation are of independent interest and will be applicable in a variety of contexts.
With that tool in hand, establishing a general framework of
bundles of
C^{*}
-correspondences
and of
equivariant correspondences
that generalises notions appearing in
[
46
,
25
]
and
[
23
]
, we construct Banach categories of
Fourier–Stieltjes
and
Fourier multipliers
between twisted actions of a fixed groupoid.
This is a far reaching generalisation of the first picture
(1)
mentioned above for groups.
We prove three different
absorption results
to achieve our goals.
The first of them implies that the Fourier category is an
ideal
in the Fourier–Stieltjes category.
The remaining two provide different generalisations of the classical
Fell’s absorption principle
for groups.
The first implies that Fourier–Stieltjes multipliers act as completely bounded maps both on the reduced and universal crossed product algebras, which provides functors into the respective categories.
The second one ensures that the Fourier multipliers even act ‘reduced-to-full’.
In the context of group actions similar versions of Fell’s absorption principle were proved in
[
6
,
7
]
.
For groupoid actions our second Fell’s absorption principle is closely related, but not exactly comparable with, the corresponding recent results in
[
39
]
and
[
16
]
.

Having proved the above results we then focus on
(3)
— the third of the pictures mentioned in the beginning of the introduction (as finding the right generalisation of the second, ‘functional’ avatar is problematic even in the context of group crossed products, as can be seen by looking at
[
54
, Section 7.6]
).
In order to obtain the desired equivalence we prove a dilation result, generalising simultaneously the KSGNS construction of Kasparov (
[
45
]
) and Murphy’s Kolmogorov decomposition of positive kernels for Hilbert modules from
[
49
]
, and which might be of interest for its own sake.
Using this we prove a core result of the paper.
It states that for any two twisted actions
(\alpha,u_{\alpha})
and
(\beta,u_{\beta})
of a given étale groupoid
\mathcal{G}
we have natural bijections between the following objects:

(1)

strict, bounded and positive-definite multipliers from
(\alpha,u_{\alpha})
to
(\beta,u_{\beta})
;

(2)

strict completely positive fibre preserving maps between the reduced crossed products;

(3)

strict completely positive fibre preserving maps between the full crossed products;

(4)

Fourier–Stieltjes multipliers from
(\alpha,u_{\alpha})
to
(\beta,u_{\beta})
of the form
T_{\mathcal{E},L,\xi,\xi}
for some equivariant correspondence
(L,\mathcal{E})
and a section
\xi\in\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{E}))
.

This together with the standard polarization formula applied to
(4)
gives the appropriate generalisation of the equivalence between the guises
(1)
and
(3)
.
The strictness of completely positive maps appearing in conditions
(1)
–
(3)
above is a very weak replacement for unitality of the algebras and maps in question.
In fact, for the approximation properties that we study strictness plays no role, as we can always conjugate with approximate units to ‘strictify’ a given map.
Our categorical approach allows us to investigate
approximation properties
of arbitrary multipliers.
When applied to the identity this leads to a fruitful interaction between properties of the Fourier–Stieltjes algebra and the approximation properties of the corresponding crossed product algebra.
For example, we provide natural approximation conditions guaranteeing that the relevant universal and reduced crossed product
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebras coincide and are nuclear.

We also return to the Haagerup trick.
For general groupoids and general cp maps it is not completely clear what is the right replacement for this device, so we decided to consider two cases where we know that it works (this is reflected in Figure
1
by the use of thinner arrows).
Namely, we assume that either the groupoid is discrete (which is still interesting, as for example it covers the case of discrete groups) or the maps in question have a certain bimodule property.
Then we can use it to characterise the relevant approximation properties, and in the discrete case to identify the natural Fourier–Stieltjes norm with the decomposable norm of
[
35
]
of the associated decomposable map, as well as give full characterisations of nuclearity of the associated crossed products.

The construction of the Fourier–Stieltjes category in the generality described above is not yet the end of the story.
One could think of further extensions to multipliers of Fell bundles, with the latter already put to use for example in
[
28
,
16
]
.
On the other hand, even the context of twisted crossed products given by groupoid actions offers many new perspectives.
These are on one hand related to the analysis of more subtle approximation properties, and on the other to potential reconstruction results, related to the question: to what extent does the Fourier–Stieltjes or Fourier algebra remember the underlying action?
At the end of the text we indicate several related avenues of future research and mention also specific problems left open in our work.

The specific plan of the paper is as follows: in Section
1
, we introduce multiplier modules,
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondences and Hilbert bimodules.
In Section
2
we prove a dilation result for a particular form of ‘positive kernels’.
Next, in Section
3
, we return to correspondences in the context of bundles over a locally compact space and describe direct sums, tensor products and natural morphisms between the respective bundles.
Section
4
passes to Fell bundles, and specifically to those which arise from twisted groupoid actions and whose cross sectional
C^{*}
-algebras give the relevant crossed products.
Here we also connect the latter to twisted inverse semigroup actions in the sense of
[
15
]
.
In Section
5
we focus on equivariant correspondences for a pair of twisted groupoid actions, setting up the appropriate framework and proving that an
equivariant correspondence induces
representations from one crossed product to another.
The process of inducing representations from correspondences without equivariant action is introduced in Section
6
.
Such representations are called
induced regular
because they always descend to reduced crossed products, while for equivariant induction this holds only if the equivariant representation is regular or if the representation from which we induce is regular.
Our two counterparts of Fell’s absorption principle describe the fundamental relationships between the two types of induction.
Section
7
uses the stage set up in the first part of the paper to finally introduce the Fourier–Stieltjes Banach category and prove that Fourier–Stieltjes multipliers act both on reduced and full crossed product
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebras.
The following Section
8
specialises to Fourier–Stieltjes multipliers of positive type; here we also establish the advertised equivalence between different points of view on such multipliers.
A short Section
9
treats Fourier multipliers, using earlier absorption theorems to deduce that they form an ideal in the class of Fourier–Stieltjes multipliers and extend to reduced-to-full maps.
Section
10
introduces the concepts of a Fourier and Fourier–Stieltjes approximation of a given multiplier and uses it to provide applications to approximation properties of actions and the resulting crossed products.
In Section
11
we discuss appropriate versions of the Haagerup trick, apply them to find other descriptions of approximation properties, as well as to provide an alternative view of the Fourier–Stieltjes norm, which in turn again has certain consequences for the approximation properties.
Finally, in Section
12
, we set up open problems and present further research perspectives.

Notation:
We will introduce the notation gradually throughout the text, but the most important conventions are as follows.
We denote by
X
a locally compact Hausdorff space and by
\mathcal{G}
a Hausdorff étale groupoid with the unit space
X
.

C^{*}
-algebras are denoted by
A
,
B
,
\dots
, Fell bundles by
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
,
\dots
, modules by
E
,
F
, and bundles of modules by
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{F}
.
If
p
is a logical statement — mostly an equality — then we use the symbol
[p]
to mean the boolean logical value of
p
, that is
[p]=1
if
p
is true and
[p]=0
if
p
is false.

1.
Multiplier C*-correspondences

In this section we discuss the notion of multiplier Banach bimodules, multiplier
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondences and multiplier Hilbert bimodules, extending the theory from
[
21
]
.

1.1.
Multiplier Banach bimodules

Throughout this paper
A
and
B
will be
\mathrm{\mathrm{C}^{*}}
-algebras
(although in this particular subsection it suffices to assume that
A
and
B
are Banach algebras with contractive approximate units).
For Banach spaces
E
,
F
, we denote by
\mathbb{B}(E,F)
the space of bounded operators
E\to F
.
Let
E
be a Banach
A
-
B
-bimodule.
In this paper Banach modules, by definition, will be
nondegenerate
,
i.e. we always assume that
AE=EB=E
.
Note that, by the Cohen–Hewitt factorisation theorem, these products may be viewed either purely algebraically or as closed linear spans.
Then
E
is also naturally a Banach
\mathcal{M}(A)
-
\mathcal{M}(B)
-bimodule,
where
\mathcal{M}(A)
and
\mathcal{M}(B)
denote the multiplier algebras of
A
and
B
, respectively.
We recall the definition of the multiplier bimodule from
[
21
]

(note that
[
21
]
considers
A
-bimodules, but the constructions and results there remain valid for
A
-
B
-bimodules).

Namely, the direct sum
\mathbb{B}(A,E)\oplus\mathbb{B}(B,E)
, equipped with the norm

\|(R,L)\|_{\infty}=\max\{\|R\|,\|L\|\},

becomes a Banach
\mathcal{M}(A)
-
\mathcal{M}(B)
-bimodule
with module actions given by

(aR)(a_{0})=R(a_{0}a),\quad(Rb)(a_{0})=R(a_{0})b,\quad(Lb)(b_{0})=L(bb_{0}),\quad(aL)(b_{0})=aL(b_{0}),

for
(R,L)\in\mathbb{B}(A,E)\oplus\mathbb{B}(B,E)
,
a_{0}\in A
,
b_{0}\in B
, and
a\in\mathcal{M}(A)
,
b\in\mathcal{M}(B)
.
A
multiplier
for
E
is a pair of maps
(R,L)
, where
R:A\to E
and
L:B\to E
, satisfying

a_{0}L(b_{0})=R(a_{0})b_{0},\qquad a_{0}\in A,\ b_{0}\in B.

By
[
21
, Proposition 2.5]
, the set of all multipliers for
E
, denoted
\mathcal{M}(E)
,
is a closed
\mathcal{M}(A)
-
\mathcal{M}(B)
-sub-bimodule of
\mathbb{B}(A,E)\oplus\mathbb{B}(B,E)
.

We have an isometric
\mathcal{M}(A)
-
\mathcal{M}(B)
-bimodule map

E\ni\xi\longmapsto(R_{\xi},L_{\xi}),\qquad R_{\xi}(a):=a\xi,\ L_{\xi}(b):=\xi b,

which allows us to view
E
as a sub-bimodule of
\mathcal{M}(E)
.
We call the
\mathcal{M}(A)
-
\mathcal{M}(B)
-bimodule
\mathcal{M}(E)
the
multiplier bimodule
of the
A
-
B
-bimodule
E
.
Below we will characterise it as the largest essential multiplier extension of
E
.

Definition 1.1
.

We say that an
\mathcal{M}(A)
-
\mathcal{M}(B)
-bimodule
F
is a
multiplier extension
of an
A
-
B
-bimodule
E
if
E\subseteq F
is a submodule such that
AF=FB=E
.
We say that this extension is
essential
if for every
\xi\in F
, we have
A\xi=0
only if
\xi=0
or equivalently,
\xi B=0
only if
\xi=0
(the equivalence follows from the equality
AF=FB=E
).

Remark 1.2
.

If either of the algebras
A
or
B
is unital then
F=1_{A}F=F1_{B}=E
for every multiplier extension
F
of
E
.

Lemma 1.3
.

For any
A
-
B
-bimodule
E
, the multiplier bimodule
\mathcal{M}(E)
is the largest essential multiplier extension of
E
.
More precisely,
\mathcal{M}(E)
is an essential multiplier extension of
E
and for any other multiplier extension
F
of
E
there is a unique (necessarily contractive)

A
-
B
-bimodule map
\mu:F\to\mathcal{M}(E)
that extends the identity on
E
.
Moreover,
\mu
is injective if and only if
F
is essential.

Proof.

For any
(R,L)\in\mathcal{M}(E)
and
a\in A
,
b\in B
we have
a\cdot(R,L)=(R_{R(a)},L_{R(a)})
and
(R,L)\cdot b=(R_{L(b)},L_{L(b)})
(note that
L
is a right module map and
R
is a left module map, see
[
21
, Lemma 2.1]
).
Hence
\mathcal{M}(E)
is a multiplier extension of
E
and clearly it is essential.
For any multiplier extension
F
we have the bimodule map
F\ni\xi\mapsto(R_{\xi},L_{\xi})\in\mathcal{M}(E)
where
R_{\xi}(a):=a\xi
and
L_{\xi}(b):=\xi b
, for
\xi\in F
.
By nondegeneracy of bimodules this is the unique bimodule map
F\to\mathcal{M}(E)
that restricts to the identity on
E
.
By construction it is contractive, and it is injective if and only if
F
is essential.
∎

Corollary 1.4
.

\mathcal{M}(E)
is the unique (up to an isometric isomorphism extending the identity on
E
) multiplier extension of
E
with the property that for any multiplier extension
F
of
E
there is a unique bimodule map
F\to\mathcal{M}(E)
that extends the identity
E\to E
.

Proof.

Follows from the usual universality considerations.
∎

We also need the following general morphism extension result.
It is a common generalisation of
[
21
, Theorem 2.8]
and the known fact that any contractive homomorphism
\pi:A\to\mathcal{M}(B)
which is
nondegenerate
in the sense that
\pi(A)B=B
, extends uniquely to a homomorphism
\overline{\pi}:\mathcal{M}(A)\to\mathcal{M}(B)
which is necessarily contractive.

Definition 1.5
.

A
morphism
from a Banach
A
-
B
-bimodule
E
to a Banach
C
-
D
-bimodule
F
consists of a linear map
\pi:E\to\mathcal{M}(F)
and nondegenerate homomorphisms
\pi_{A}:A\to\mathcal{M}(C)
,
\pi_{B}:B\to\mathcal{M}(D)
such that
\pi(a\xi b)=\pi_{A}(a)\pi(\xi)\pi_{B}(b)
for
\xi\in E
,
a\in A
,
b\in B
.
We say that this morphism is
contractive
if all the maps
(\pi,\pi_{A},\pi_{B})
are contractive.

Lemma 1.6
.

Any contractive morphism
(\pi,\pi_{A},\pi_{B}):E\to F
extends uniquely to a morphism
(\overline{\pi},\overline{\pi}_{A},\overline{\pi}_{B}):\mathcal{M}(E)\to\mathcal{M}(F)
which is necessarily contractive.

Proof.

Take any
(R,L)\in\mathcal{M}(E)
.
We claim that the formulas

\widetilde{R}(c\pi_{A}(a)):=c\pi(R(a)),\qquad\widetilde{L}(\pi_{B}(b)d):=\pi(L(b))d,\quad\mbox{ for $a\in A,b\in B,c\in C,d\in D$,}

yield linear maps
\widetilde{R}:C\to F
,
\widetilde{L}:D\to F
.
Indeed, letting
\{e_{i}^{A}\}_{i\in I}
and
\{e_{i}^{B}\}_{i\in I}
be approximate units respectively in
A
and
B
, we get
c\pi(R(a))=\lim_{i}c\pi(R(ae_{i}^{A}))=\lim_{i}c\pi(aR(e_{i}^{A}))=\lim_{i}c\pi_{A}(a)\pi(R(e_{i}^{A}))
and similarly
\pi_{B}(b)d=\lim_{i}\pi(L(e_{i}^{B}))\pi_{B}(b)d
.
This implies that
\widetilde{R}
and
\widetilde{L}
are well defined and contractive.
We have

\widetilde{R}(c)=\lim_{i}c\pi(R(e_{i}^{A})),\qquad\widetilde{L}(d)=\lim_{i}\pi(L(e_{i}^{B}))d,\qquad c\in C,d\in D.

Moreover, for
a\in A,b\in B,c\in C,d\in D
we get

c\pi_{A}(a)\widetilde{L}(\pi_{B}(b)d)=c\pi(aL(b))d=c\pi(R(a)b)d=c\pi(R(a))\pi_{B}(b)d=\widetilde{R}(c\pi_{A}(a))\pi_{B}(b)d.

Hence
(\widetilde{R},\widetilde{L})\in\mathcal{M}(F)
.
Thus putting
\overline{\pi}(R,L):=(\widetilde{R},\widetilde{L})
we get a contractive extension
\overline{\pi}:\mathcal{M}(E)\to\mathcal{M}(F)
of
\pi
.
It can be readily checked that for
a\in A
,
b\in B
and
\xi=(R,L)\in\mathcal{M}(E)
we have
\pi_{A}(a)\overline{\pi}(\xi)\pi_{B}(b)=\pi(a\xi b)
.
This property determines
\overline{\pi}
uniquely, as for any other
\tilde{\pi}:\mathcal{M}(E)\to\mathcal{M}(F)
with this property we have
(\overline{\pi}(\xi)-\tilde{\pi}(\xi))\pi_{B}(B)=0
which implies
(\overline{\pi}(\xi)-\tilde{\pi}(\xi))D=0
and therefore
\overline{\pi}(\xi)=\tilde{\pi}(\xi)
because
\mathcal{M}(F)
is an essential extension of
F
.

The above (in the special case where
E=A=B
and
F=C=D
are Banach algebras and
\pi=\pi_{A}=\pi_{B}
) also proves the aforementioned fact that contractive nondegenerate homomorphisms between Banach algebras extend uniquely to homomorphisms between their multiplier algebras, and they are necessarily contractive.
Thus we have unique (contractive) homomorphic extensions
\overline{\pi}_{A}:\mathcal{M}(A)\to\mathcal{M}(C)
and
\overline{\pi}_{B}:\mathcal{M}(B)\to\mathcal{M}(D)
of
\pi_{A}
and
\pi_{B}
. We immediately get that
\overline{\pi}_{A}(a)\overline{\pi}(\xi)\overline{\pi}_{B}(b)=\overline{\pi}(a\xi b)
for
a\in\mathcal{M}(A)
,
b\in\mathcal{M}(B)
and
\xi\in\mathcal{M}(E)
.
Thus
(\overline{\pi},\overline{\pi}_{A},\overline{\pi}_{B})
is a unique morphism that extends
(\pi,\pi_{A},\pi_{B})
.
∎

1.2.
Multiplier C*-correspondences

Let
E
and
F
be (right)
B
-Hilbert modules.
We use the standard notation
\mathcal{L}(E,F)
for the Banach space of adjointable operators from
E
to
F
, and
\mathcal{K}(E,F)\subseteq\mathcal{L}(E,F)
for the closed subspace of (generalised) compact operators, generated by the ‘rank-one’ operators
\Theta_{x,y}(z):=x\langle y,z\rangle_{B}
,
x\in F,y,z\in E
.
In particular,
\mathcal{K}(E):=\mathcal{K}(E,E)
is an ideal in the
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra
\mathcal{L}(E):=\mathcal{L}(E,E)
.
A
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence
from
A
to
B
(or an
A
-
B
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence) is a Hilbert module
E
over
B
together with a nondegenerate
*
-homomorphism from
A
to
\mathcal{L}(E)
, which introduces a left
A
-module structure on
E
.
In particular, an
A
-
B
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence is a Banach
A
-
B
-bimodule (where both actions are nondegenerate), so that the constructions of the previous subsection apply.
Any
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra
A
can be treated as a trivial
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence from
A
to
A
where the bimodule structure comes from
A
and the
A
-valued inner product is given by the formula
\langle a,b\rangle:=a^{*}b
,
a,b\in A
. Then
\mathcal{K}(A)\cong A
and
\mathcal{L}(A)=\mathcal{M}(A)
is the multiplier algebra of
A
.
For any
A
-
B
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence
E
the actions extend uniquely to multiplier algebras so that we may always treat
E
as a
\mathcal{M}(A)
-
\mathcal{M}(B)
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence.
To relate it to the multiplier
\mathcal{M}(A)
-
\mathcal{M}(B)
-bimodule
\mathcal{M}(E)
, let us note that whenever
E_{B}
is a
B
-Hilbert module, then
\mathcal{L}(E_{B},E)
is naturally a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence from
\mathcal{M}(A)
to
\mathcal{L}(E_{B})
where

(aT)(x):=aT(x),\qquad(Tb)(x):=Tb(x),\qquad\langle S,T\rangle_{\mathcal{L}(E_{B})}:=S^{*}T,

for
S,T\in\mathcal{L}(E_{B},E)
,
a\in M(A)
,
b\in\mathcal{L}(E_{B})
,
x\in E_{B}
.
In particular,
\mathcal{L}(E_{B},E)
contains
\mathcal{K}(E_{B},E)
as a sub-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence.
If
E_{B}=B
is the trivial
B
-Hilbert module, then
\mathcal{L}(B,E)
is a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence from
\mathcal{M}(A)
to
\mathcal{M}(B)=\mathcal{L}(B)
and
\mathcal{K}(B,E)
is naturally isomorphic to
E
; the isomorphism is determined by the map
\Theta_{\xi,b}\mapsto\xi b^{*}
,
\xi\in E,b\in B
.
Under the identifications
B=\mathcal{K}(B)
and
E=\mathcal{K}(B,E)
we have
\mathcal{L}(B,E)\cdot B=E
, but in general
A\cdot\mathcal{L}(B,E)
is not contained in
E
, unless for instance
E
is
proper
, i.e.
A
acts on
E
by compact operators.
Hence in general
\mathcal{L}(B,E)
is not a multiplier extension of
E
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.
In fact,

\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}^{*}}(E):=\{T\in\mathcal{L}(B,E):aT\in\mathcal{K}(B,E)\text{ for all }a\in A\}

is the largest sub-bimodule of
\mathcal{L}(B,E)
which is a multiplier extension of
E=\mathcal{K}(B,E)
.

Lemma 1.7
.

For any
A
-
B
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence
E
, the projection
\mathcal{M}(E)\ni(R,L)\mapsto L\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}^{*}}(E)
is an isomorphism of Banach
\mathcal{M}(A)
-
\mathcal{M}(B)
-bimodules.

Proof.

If
(R,L)\in\mathcal{M}(E)
, then
L
is adjointable and
L^{*}(a\xi)=\langle R(a^{*}),\xi\rangle_{B}
, for
a\in A
and
\xi\in E
, because for any
b\in B
we have

\langle L(b),a\xi\rangle_{B}=\langle a^{*}L(b),\xi\rangle_{B}=\langle R(a^{*})b,\xi\rangle_{B}=b^{*}\langle R(a^{*}),\xi\rangle_{B}=\langle b,\langle R(a^{*}),\xi\rangle_{B}\rangle_{B}.

Thus
R
is uniquely determined by
L
and
\|R\|\leq\|L^{*}\|=\|L\|
.
Also for any
a\in A
, writing
R(a)=\xi b^{*}
for
\xi\in E
and
b\in B
we get that

aL=\Theta_{\xi,b}
.
Thus
L\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}^{*}}(E)
and we see that
\mathcal{M}(E)\ni(R,L)\mapsto L\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}^{*}}(E)
is an isometric embedding of bimodules.
To show it is onto let
L\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}^{*}}(E)
.
For any
a\in A
,
aL\in\mathcal{K}(B,E)
and therefore for any approximate unit
\{e_{i}\}_{i\in I}
in
B
the limit
\lim_{i\in I}aL(e_{i})\in E
exists (because
aL
can be approximated in the norm by a finite sum
\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Theta_{\xi_{k},b_{k}}
and
\lim_{i\in I}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Theta_{\xi_{k},b_{k}}(e_{i})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\xi_{k}b_{k}^{*}\in E
).
Putting
R(a):=\lim_{i}aL(e_{i})
one gets a multiplier
(R,L)
.
We leave the remaining straightforward details to the reader.
∎

In the sequel we shall use the above lemma to identify
\mathcal{M}(E)=\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}^{*}}(E)\subseteq\mathcal{L}(B,E)
.
The advantage is that then we have also the following equality

\mathcal{M}(E)=\{T\in\mathcal{L}(B,E):\langle S,aT\rangle_{\mathcal{M}(B)}\in B\text{ for }a\in A,\,S\in\mathcal{L}(B,E)\},

see for instance
[
38
, Proposition 1.3]
, which readily implies that
\mathcal{M}(E)
is in fact a sub-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence of the
\mathcal{M}(A)
-
\mathcal{M}(B)
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence
\mathcal{L}(B,E)
.

Remark 1.8
.

A
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence multiplier extension of an
A
-
B
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence
E
is a
\mathcal{M}(A)
-
\mathcal{M}(B)
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence
F
such that
E=FB
and

\langle\xi,a\eta\rangle_{\mathcal{M}(B)}\in B,\qquad\text{ for all }a\in A,\ \xi,\eta\in F,

because the displayed condition is equivalent to
AF=FB
, by
[
38
, Proposition 1.3]
.
Any
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence multiplier extension is automatically essential.

Proposition 1.9
.

The multiplier bimodule
\mathcal{M}(E)
of an
A
-
B
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence
E
has a uniquely determined structure of an
\mathcal{M}(A)
-
\mathcal{M}(B)
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence extending the one from
E
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{M}(E)
is the largest among
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondences which are multiplier extensions of
E
.
Namely, for any
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence multiplier extension
F
of
E
, the unique bimodule map
F\to\mathcal{M}(E)
that extends the identity on
E
is necessarily an isometry (preserves inner products).

Proof.

We already observed that
\mathcal{M}(E)
has the
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence structure inherited from
\mathcal{L}(B,E)
.
By Lemma
1.3
, the Banach
A
-
B
-bimodule structure of
\mathcal{M}(E)
uniquely extends the one from
E
, and the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{M}(B)}
in
\mathcal{M}(E)
is determined by the one
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{B}
in
E
because for any approximate unit
\{e_{i}\}_{i\in I}
in
B
, and any
\xi,\eta\in F
we have

\langle\xi,\eta\rangle_{\mathcal{M}(B)}=\text{s-}\!\lim_{i,j\in I}e_{j}\langle\xi,\eta\rangle_{\mathcal{M}(B)}e_{i}=\text{s-}\!\lim_{i,j\in I}\langle\xi e_{j},\eta e_{i}\rangle_{B},

where
\text{s-}\!\lim
denotes the strict limit.
Let
F
satisfy the conditions in the second part of the assertion.
Then
F
is an essential multiplier extension of
E
, and so the identity on
E
uniquely extends to a bimodule map
u:F\to\mathcal{M}(E)
by Lemma
1.3
.
It necessarily preserves the inner products as for an approximate unit
\{e_{i}\}_{i\in I}
in
B
, and
\xi,\eta\in F
, as above we have
\langle u\xi,u\eta\rangle_{\mathcal{M}(B)}=\text{s-}\!\lim_{i,j\in I}e_{j}\langle u(\xi),u(\eta)\rangle_{\mathcal{M}(B)}e_{i}=\text{s-}\!\lim_{i,j\in I}\langle u(\xi e_{j}),u(\eta e_{i})\rangle_{B}=\text{s-}\!\lim_{i,j\in I}\langle\xi e_{j},\eta e_{i}\rangle_{B}=\langle\xi,\eta\rangle_{\mathcal{M}(B)}
.
∎

Remark 1.10
.

An
A
-
B
-
Hilbert bimodule
is an
A
-
B
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence
E
which is also a left
A
-Hilbert module and the inner products satisfy
{{}_{A}\langle}\xi,\zeta\rangle\cdot\eta=\xi\cdot\langle\zeta,\eta\rangle_{B}
,
\xi,\zeta,\eta\in E
.
Equivalently,
E
is an
A
-
B
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence such that the left action
\varphi:A\to\mathcal{L}(E)
restricts to a
*
-isomorphism
I\cong\mathcal{K}(E)
where
I
is an ideal in
A
(we then necessarily have
I=(\ker\varphi)^{\bot}
).
If
E
is an
A
-
B
-Hilbert bimodule, then
M(E)
is
\mathcal{M}(A)
-
\mathcal{M}(B)
-Hilbert bimodule that agrees with the multiplier Hilbert bimodule constructed in
[
62
]
, and earlier for equivalence bimodules in
[
26
]
.
In particular, Proposition
1.9
together with Corollary
1.4
, generalise and improve
[
62
, Proposition 3.6]
,
[
26
, Proposition 1.2]
.

Example 1.11

(Matrix
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondences)
.

Consider a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-
A
-
B
-correspondence
E
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then, as described in
[
10
, Section 3]
, the space

\mathbb{M}_{n}(E):=\{[\xi_{ij}]_{i,j=1}^{n}:\xi_{ij}\in E,\ i,j=1,\ldots,n\}.

becomes a Hilbert
\mathbb{M}_{n}(B)
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-module with the natural right matrix multiplication and the inner product of
\xi=[\xi_{ij}]_{i,j=1}^{n}
,
\zeta=[\zeta_{ij}]_{i,j=1}^{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(E)
given by

\langle\xi,\zeta\rangle_{M_{n}(B)}=\left[\sum_{k=1}^{n}\langle\xi_{ki},\zeta_{kj}\rangle_{B}\right]_{i,j=1,\ldots,n}.

If the left action of
A
on
E
is given by
\phi:A\to\mathcal{L}(E)
, then the left action of
\mathbb{M}_{n}(A)
on
\mathbb{M}_{n}(E)
is given by
\phi^{(n)}:\mathbb{M}_{n}(A)\to\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathcal{L}(E))\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{M}_{n}(E))
, where the last inclusion is given by
T=[T_{ij}]_{i,j=1}^{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathcal{L}(E))
and
\xi\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(E)
is as above, then

T\xi=\left[\sum_{k=1}^{n}T_{ik}(\xi_{kj})\right]_{i,j=1,\ldots,n}.

Furthermore, we have the expected identification
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{M}_{n}(E))\cong\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathcal{M}(E))
, compatible with the standard isomorphisms
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{M}_{n}(A))\cong\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathcal{M}(A))
,
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{M}_{n}(B))\cong\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathcal{M}(B))
.
This is easiest to verify via Corollary
1.4
.
Indeed, if
F
is a multiplier extension of the
\mathbb{M}_{n}(A)
-
\mathbb{M}_{n}(B)
-bimodule
\mathbb{M}_{n}(E)
, the desired map
F\to\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathcal{M}(E))
can be constructed from entrywise embeddings
\psi_{ij}:E\to\mathbb{M}_{n}(E)\subseteq F
, which are compatible with the ‘diagonal’ actions of
A
and
B
— so that via the universal property of
\mathcal{M}(E)
one obtains unique maps
\gamma_{ij}:F\to E
.
Naturally combining these yields a bimodule map
\gamma:F\to\mathbb{M}_{n}(E)
which is a unique extension of the identity.

Let
E
and
F
be
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondences from
A
to
B
and from
B
to
C
, respectively.
The (internal)
tensor product

E\otimes_{B}F
is a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence from
A
to
C
constructed as the completion of the algebraic tensor product of
E
and
F
balanced over
B
, with inner product determined by

\langle\xi_{1}\otimes\zeta_{1},\xi_{2}\otimes\zeta_{2}\rangle_{C}=\langle\zeta_{1},\langle\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\rangle_{B}\cdot\zeta_{2}\rangle_{C},

\xi_{i}\in E
,
\zeta_{i}\in F
,
i=1,2
.
This readily implies that we have a natural
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence isomorphism
\mathcal{M}(E)\otimes_{\mathcal{M}(B)}F\cong E\otimes_{B}F
that sends
\xi\otimes b\zeta
to
\xi b\otimes\zeta
for
\xi\in\mathcal{M}(E)
,
\zeta\in F
and
b\in B
.
Similarly, we also have the isomorphism
E\otimes_{\mathcal{M}(B)}\mathcal{M}(F)\cong E\otimes_{B}F
. Therefore we may and we will assume the identifications

E\otimes_{B}F=\mathcal{M}(E)\otimes_{\mathcal{M}(B)}F=E\otimes_{\mathcal{M}(B)}\mathcal{M}(F)\subseteq\mathcal{M}(E)\otimes_{\mathcal{M}(B)}\mathcal{M}(F).

Then
\mathcal{M}(E)\otimes_{\mathcal{M}(B)}\mathcal{M}(F)
is clearly a multiplier extension of
E\otimes_{B}F
.

Lemma 1.12
.

For
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondences
E
from
A
to
B
and
F
from
B
to
C
we have an embedding of
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondences

\mathcal{M}(E)\otimes_{\mathcal{M}(B)}\mathcal{M}(F)\rightarrowtail\mathcal{M}(E\otimes_{B}F)

which is given by the unique bimodule map that extends the identity on
E\otimes_{B}F
.
More specifically, the formula
(\xi\otimes\zeta)(c):=\xi\otimes\zeta(c)\in\mathcal{M}(E)\otimes_{\mathcal{M}(B)}F=E\otimes_{B}F
for
c\in C,\xi\in\mathcal{M}(E)
and
\zeta\in\mathcal{M}(F)
defines an operator
\xi\otimes\zeta\in\mathcal{L}(C,E\otimes_{B}F)
and this gives a map

\mathcal{M}(E)\times\mathcal{M}(F)\ni(\xi,\zeta)\longmapsto\xi\otimes\zeta\in\mathcal{M}(E\otimes_{B}F)\subseteq\mathcal{L}(C,E\otimes_{B}F)

that induces the aforementioned embedding.

Proof.

The first part follows from Proposition
1.9
.
For the second part, note that the operator
\xi\otimes\zeta
, for
\xi\in\mathcal{M}(E)
,
\zeta\in\mathcal{M}(F)
is adjointable, where
(\xi\otimes\zeta)^{*}(\xi_{0}\otimes\zeta_{0})=\zeta^{*}(\langle\xi,\xi_{0}\rangle_{\mathcal{M}(B)}\zeta_{0})
for
\xi_{0}\in\mathcal{M}(E)
,
\zeta_{0}\in\mathcal{M}(F)
.
Hence
\xi\otimes\zeta\in\mathcal{L}(C,E\otimes_{B}F)
.
Clearly, for
b\in B
we have
\xi b\otimes\zeta=\xi\otimes b\zeta\in E\otimes_{B}F
.
In particular, for any
a\in A
we have
a(\xi\otimes\zeta)=(a\xi\otimes\zeta)\in E\otimes_{\mathcal{M}(B)}\mathcal{M}(F)=E\otimes_{B}F
.
Thus the map
\mathcal{M}(E)\times\mathcal{M}(F)\ni(\xi,\zeta)\longmapsto\xi\otimes\zeta\in\mathcal{M}(E\otimes_{B}F)
is well defined. Since it is bilinear, bimodule and
B
-balanced, it descends to a bimodule map on the algebraic
B
-balanced tensor product of
\mathcal{M}(E)
and
\mathcal{M}(F)
, which in turn extends to the embedding
\mathcal{M}(E)\otimes_{\mathcal{M}(B)}\mathcal{M}(F)\rightarrowtail\mathcal{M}(E\otimes_{B}F)
by the first part of the assertion.
∎

Remark 1.13
.

The above embedding is, in general, not surjective.
For instance, let
A
be a non-unital
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra, and let
E=A
and
F=A
be viewed as
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondences from
A
to
\mathcal{M}(A)
and from
\mathcal{M}(A)
to
A
, respectively, so that
M(E)=M(F)=A
.
Then
\mathcal{M}(E)\otimes_{\mathcal{M}(A)}\mathcal{M}(F)=E\otimes_{\mathcal{M}(A)}F=A\subsetneq\mathcal{M}(A)=\mathcal{M}(E\otimes_{\mathcal{M}(A)}F)
as
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondences from
\mathcal{M}(A)
to
\mathcal{M}(A)
.

2.
Dilation/Decomposition theorem

Let
A,B
be
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebras.
In the sequel we will study maps
T:A\to B
that are of the form
T(a)=\langle\xi,a\zeta\rangle_{\mathcal{M}(B)}=\xi^{*}a\zeta
for
\xi,\zeta\in\mathcal{M}(E)\subseteq\mathcal{L}(B,E)
, with
E
a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence from
A
to
B
.
To this end, we develop in this section the following common generalisation of Kasparov’s Stinespring dilation theorem (called the KSGNS construction in
[
45
, Theorem 5.6]
) and Murphy’s Kolmogorov decomposition of positive kernels for Hilbert modules
[
49
, Theorem 2.3]
.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
k:S\times S\to\mathbb{B}(A,\mathcal{L}(E_{B}))
be a map where
A
and
B
are
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebras,
E_{B}
is a right
B
-Hilbert module, and
S
is a set.
The following are equivalent:

(1)

there exists an
A
-
B
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence
E
and a map
\zeta:S\to\mathcal{L}(E_{B},E)
such that

k(s,t)(a)=\zeta(s)^{*}\varphi_{E}(a)\zeta(t),\qquad s,t\in S,\ a\in A,

where
\varphi_{E}:A\to\mathcal{L}(E)
is the
∗
-homomorphism defining the left action of
A
on
E
.

(2)

k
is a
positive-definite kernel
in the sense that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\in S
,
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in A
and
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{n}\in E_{B}
, we have

\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\langle\xi_{i},k(s_{i},s_{j})(a_{i}^{*}a_{j})\xi_{j}\rangle_{B}\geq 0,

and in addition
k
is
strict
in the sense that for each
s\in S
the map
k(s,s):A\to\mathcal{L}(E_{B})
is strict, i.e. for an approximate unit
\{e_{i}\}_{i\in I}
in
A
, the net
\{k(s,s)(e_{i})\}_{i\in I}
converges strictly in
\mathcal{L}(E_{B})
.

If the above conditions hold, the
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence
E
in
(1)
may be chosen so that
\{a\zeta(s)\xi:a\in A,s\in S,\xi\in E_{B}\}
is linearly dense in
E
; we call such pairs
(\zeta,E)

minimal
.
Given another minimal pair
(\zeta^{\prime},E^{\prime})
as above we have a uniquely determined unitary isomorphism of
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondences
U:E\to E^{\prime}
such that
U\zeta(s)=\zeta^{\prime}(s)
,
s\in S
.

Proof.

Assume
(1)
.
Using the notation in
(2)
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\langle\xi_{i},k(s_{i},s_{j})(a_{i}^{*}a_{j})\xi_{j}\rangle_{B}

\displaystyle=\langle\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\zeta(s_{i})\xi_{i},\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\zeta(s_{i})\xi_{i}\rangle_{B}\geq 0,

so
k
is positive-definite.
Also for any
\xi\in E_{B}
,
s\in S
and an approximate unit
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
of
A
, the net
k(s,s)(e_{i})\xi=\zeta(s)^{*}e_{i}\zeta(s)\xi
converges to
\zeta(s)^{*}\zeta(s)\xi
.
Hence
k
is strict.

Now assume
(2)
.
Begin by defining a new positive-definite kernel, now in the usual sense of
[
49
]
,
\tilde{k}:(S\times A)\times(S\times A)\to\mathcal{L}(E_{B})
, as follows:

\tilde{k}((s,a),(t,b)):=k(s,t)(a^{*}b),\qquad s,t\in S,\ a,b\in A.

By
[
49
, Theorem 2.3]
there exists a Hilbert
B
-module
E
and a map
\tilde{\zeta}:S\times A\to\mathcal{L}(E_{B},E)
(with
\|\tilde{\zeta}(s,a)\|\leq\|k(s,s)\|^{1/2}\|a\|
for all
s\in S,a\in A
) such that for each
s,t\in S,a,b\in A
we have

(2.1)

k(s,t)(a^{*}b)=\tilde{k}((s,a),(t,b))=\tilde{\zeta}(s,a)^{*}\tilde{\zeta}(t,b).

Moreover, we may and we do assume that
\tilde{\zeta}
is minimal, i.e. the set
\bigcup_{s\in S,a\in A}\tilde{\zeta}(s,a)E_{B}
is linearly dense in
E
.
We claim that there is a natural
*
-homomorphism
\varphi_{E}:\mathcal{M}(A)\to\mathcal{L}(E)
determined by the formula

\varphi_{E}(u)\tilde{\zeta}(s,a)\xi:=\tilde{\zeta}(s,ua)\xi,\qquad u\in\mathcal{M}(A),\ s\in S,\ a\in A,\ \xi\in E_{B}.

To see that
\varphi_{E}(u)
exists, by linearity and the Russo–Dye theorem (
[
61
]
) it suffices to consider the case when
u\in\mathcal{M}(A)
is unitary.
Let then
u\in\mathcal{UM}(A)
.
It is then easy to see that the map
\tilde{\zeta}_{u}:S\times A\to\mathcal{L}(E_{B},E)
given by

\tilde{\zeta}_{u}(s,a)=\tilde{\zeta}(s,ua),\qquad s\in S,\ a\in A,

yields another minimal Kolmogorov dilation of the kernel
\tilde{k}
.
Hence, by
[
49
, Theorem 2.3]
, there exists a unitary
\varphi_{E}(u)\in\mathcal{L}(E)
such that
\varphi_{E}(u)\tilde{\zeta}(s,a)=\tilde{\zeta}(s,ua)
for all
s\in S
and
a\in A
.
It is then routine to check that the prescription
u\mapsto\varphi_{E}(u)
extends to a unital
∗
-homomorphism from
\mathcal{M}(A)
to
\mathcal{L}(E)
.
Restriction of
\varphi_{E}
to
A
gives
E
the structure of a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence from
A
to
B
.
Indeed, as
k
is bounded-operator valued, we have for any approximate unit
\{e_{i}\}_{i\in I}
in
A
,
s,t\in S
and
a,b\in A
that
(k(s,t)(be_{i}a))_{i\in I}
converges (pointwise) to
k(s,t)(ba)
.
This it suffices to verify that
\varphi_{E}|_{A}:A\to\mathcal{L}(E)
is nondegenerate.

Fix an approximate unit
\{e_{i}\}_{i\in I}
in
A
and
s\in S
.
For any
\xi\in E_{B}
and
i,j\in I,i\geq j
, using (
2.1
), we get (noting that
k(s,s)
is positive)

\|\tilde{\zeta}(s,e_{i})\xi-\tilde{\zeta}(s,e_{j})\xi\|^{2}=\|\langle\xi,k(s,s)\big((e_{i}-e_{j})^{2}\big)\xi\rangle_{B}\|\leq\|\langle\xi,k(s,s)(e_{i}-e_{j})\xi\rangle_{B}\|.

Since
\{k(s,s)(e_{i})\}_{i\in I}
is strictly Cauchy it follows that
\{\tilde{\zeta}(s,e_{i})\}_{i\in I}
is strictly Cauchy and hence it is convergent to an operator
\zeta(s)\in\mathcal{L}(E_{B},E)
.
In this way we get a map
\zeta:S\to\mathcal{L}(E_{B},E)
, and for any
a\in A

k(s,t)(a)=\lim_{i,j\in I}k(s,t)(e_{i}ae_{j})=\lim_{i,j\in I}\tilde{\zeta}(s,e_{i})^{*}\varphi_{E}(a)\tilde{\zeta}(t,e_{j})=\zeta(s)^{*}\varphi_{E}(a)\zeta(t).

This proves
(1)
.
Once again using the convergence of
\{k(s,t)(ae_{i}b)\}_{i\in I}
to
k(s,t)(ab)
we deduce that
a\zeta(s)\xi=\tilde{\zeta}(s,a)\xi
and hence the pair
(\zeta,E)
is minimal in the sense described in the last part of the assertion.

Suppose now that we are given another minimal pair
(\zeta^{\prime},E^{\prime})
that dilates
k
as in
(1)
.
Putting
\tilde{\zeta^{\prime}}(s,a):=a\cdot\zeta^{\prime}(s)
for all
s\in S
,
a\in A
,
we get a map
\tilde{\zeta^{\prime}}:S\times A\to E^{\prime}
which is a minimal Kolmogorov decomposition for the kernel
\tilde{k}
.
So once again we have a unique unitary
U\in\mathcal{L}(E;E^{\prime})
such that
U(\tilde{\zeta}(s,a))=\tilde{\zeta^{\prime}}(s,a)
for all
s\in S
,
a\in A
.
It is routine to check that such a
U
necessarily intertwines the respective left actions of
A
on
E
and
E^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 2.2
.

When
S
is a singleton, Theorem
2.1
reduces to the KSGNS dilation of strict completely positive maps
\varrho:A\to\mathcal{L}(E_{B})
,
[
45
, Theorem 5.6]
.
When
A=\mathbb{C}
is trivial, then under the identification
\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{C},\mathcal{L}(E_{B}))\cong\mathcal{L}(E_{B})
, via the map
\mathbb{B}(\mathbb{C},\mathcal{L}(E_{B}))\ni T\mapsto T(1)\in\mathcal{L}(E_{B})
, Theorem
2.1
reduces to Murphy’s Kolmogorov decomposition,
[
49
, Theorem 2.3]
(which was used in the proof above).

Remark 2.3
.

If
k(s,s)\in\mathbb{B}(A,\mathcal{K}(E_{B}))
for all
s\in S
, then for any (minimal) decomposition
\zeta:S\to\mathcal{L}(E_{B},E)
as in Theorem
2.1
and any
a\in A
we have
\varphi_{E}(a)\zeta:S\to\mathcal{K}(E_{B},E)
.
This follows for instance from
[
38
, Proposition 1.3]
applied to each
\varphi_{E}(a)\zeta(s)
.
In particular, if the kernel in Murphy’s theorem takes values in compact operators
\mathcal{K}(E_{B})
, then its Kolmogorov decomposition is also compact valued.

We will need yet another version of dilation that follows from Theorem
2.1
.

Corollary 2.4
.

Let
k:S\times S\to\mathbb{B}(A,B)
be a map where
A
and
B
are
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebras and
S
is a set.
The following are equivalent:

(1)

there exists a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence
E
from
A
to
B
and a map
\zeta:S\to\mathcal{M}(E)
such that

k(s,t)(a)=\langle\zeta(s),a\cdot\zeta(t)\rangle_{\mathcal{M}(B)},\qquad s,t\in S,\ a\in A;

(2)

k
is a
positive-definite kernel
in the sense that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\in S
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in A
the matrix

[k(s_{i},s_{j})(a_{i}^{*}a_{j})]_{i,j=1}^{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(B)

is positive, and
k
is
strict
in the sense that for each
s\in S
the map
k(s,s):A\to B
is strict, i.e. for an approximate unit
\{e_{i}\}_{i\in I}
in
A
, the net
\{k(s,s)(e_{i})\}_{i\in I}
converges strictly in
\mathcal{M}(B)
.

Moreover, if the conditions above hold, then the
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence
E
in
(1)
may be chosen so that the set
\{a\zeta(s)b:s\in S,a\in A,b\in B\}
is dense in
E
; we call such pairs
(\zeta,E)

minimal
.
Given another minimal pair
(\zeta^{\prime},E^{\prime})
as above, we have a uniquely determined unitary isomorphism of
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondences
U:E\to E^{\prime}
such that
U\zeta(s)=\zeta^{\prime}(s)
,
s\in S
.

Proof.

Let
E_{B}:=B
be the trivial Hilbert module over
B
, so that
B\cong\mathcal{K}(E_{B})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(E_{B})
.
Then clearly
(2)
in Theorem
2.1
and
(2)
in Corollary
2.4
are equivalent.
Also by Remark
2.3
, one readily sees that
(1)
in Theorem
2.1
is equivalent to
(1)
in Corollary
2.4
.
∎

3.
Bundles of C*-correspondences

Throughout this paper
X
will stand for a locally compact Hausdorff space.
In this section we introduce bundles of
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondences between two
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-bundles over
X
and present several relevant constructions.

A
Banach bundle

\mathcal{E}
over
X
is a topological space with an open continuous surjection
p:\mathcal{E}\to X
such that each fibre
E_{x}=p^{-1}(x)
is a Banach space whose topology agrees with that of
\mathcal{E}
, the linear operations are continuous, and the norm
\mathcal{E}\ni a\mapsto\|a\|\in[0,\infty)
is
upper semicontinuous
and nondegenerate, see, for instance,
[
15
, Definition 2.1]
for a detailed definition.
If the norm is continuous then we say
\mathcal{E}
is a
continuous Banach bundle
.
We will often write
\mathcal{E}=\{E_{x}\}_{x\in X}
for a Banach bundle.
We denote by
\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{E})
,
\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{E})
,
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{E})
the spaces of continuous sections that are compactly supported, vanishing at infinity, and bounded, respectively.
By
Fell’s reconstruction theorem
, any of these spaces is enough to reconstruct the topology of
\mathcal{E}
.
More specifically (see
[
31
, Theorem II.13.18]
or
[
15
, Proposition 2.4]
), for any family of Banach spaces
\mathcal{E}=\{E_{x}\}_{x\in X}
and any linear space
\Gamma
of sections of
\mathcal{E}
such that
\overline{\{\xi(x):\xi\in\Gamma\}}=E_{x}
for each
x\in X
and
X\ni x\mapsto\|\xi(x)\|\in[0,\infty)
is upper semicontinuous for each
\xi\in\Gamma
, there exists a unique topology on
\mathcal{E}
turning it into a Banach bundle with
\Gamma\subseteq C(\mathcal{E})
.
A net
\{f_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of elements of
\mathcal{E}
converges in this topology to
f\in\mathcal{E}
if and only if
\lim_{i\in I}p(f_{i})=p(f)
and for every
\varepsilon>0
there is
\xi\in\Gamma
such that
\|f-\xi(p(f))\|<\varepsilon
and
\|f_{i}-\xi(p(f_{i}))\|<\varepsilon
for large enough
i\in I
.

It is well known that (upper semicontinuous) bundles of
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebras
\mathcal{A}=\{A_{x}\}_{x\in X}
are equivalent to
\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebras, see, for instance,
[
70
, Appendix C]
.
More specifically, a
\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra is a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra
A
equipped with a nondegenerate homomorphism
\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)\to Z(\mathcal{M}(A))
turning
A
into a
\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
-module.
Given a bundle of
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebras
\mathcal{A}
we write
A=\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A})
for its
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra of
\mathrm{C_{0}}
-sections, equipped with the
\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
-module structure given by

(fa)(x)=f(x)a(x),\qquad f\in\mathrm{C_{0}}(X),a\in\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A}).

We now briefly explain how this equivalence extends to
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondences.
We begin by introducing a bundle version of Le Gall’s notion of a
\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
-representation
[
46
, Definition 4.1]
, which was renamed a
\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence
by Deaconu in
[
23
, Definition 5.8]
.

Definition 3.1
.

A
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence bundle
from a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-bundle
\mathcal{A}=\{A_{x}\}_{x\in X}
to a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-bundle
\mathcal{B}=\{B_{x}\}_{x\in X}
is a Banach bundle
\mathcal{E}=\{E_{x}\}_{x\in X}
, where each
E_{x}
is a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence from
A_{x}
to
B_{x}
, and the sections

X\ni x\mapsto\langle\zeta(x),\xi(x)\rangle_{E_{x}}\in\mathcal{B},\qquad X\ni x\mapsto a(x)\xi(x)\in\mathcal{E},\qquad X\ni x\mapsto\xi(x)b(x)\in\mathcal{E}

are continuous for every
\zeta,\xi\in\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{E})
,
a\in\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{A})
,
b\in\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{B})
.

Proposition 3.2
.

For any
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence bundle
\mathcal{E}=\{E_{x}\}_{x\in X}
from
\mathcal{A}=\{A_{x}\}_{x\in X}
to
\mathcal{B}=\{B_{x}\}_{x\in X}
the space
E:=\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{E})
is a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence from
A:=\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A})
to
B:=\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{B})
with the additional property that

(3.1)

(fa)\cdot\xi\cdot b=a\cdot\xi\cdot(fb)\quad\text{for all $f\in\mathrm{C_{0}}(X),\ a\in A,\ b\in B,\ \xi\in E$,}

i.e.
E
is a
\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence from
A
to
B
in the sense of
[
23
]
.
Moreover, every
\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence from
A
to
B
is of the above form for some
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence bundle
\mathcal{E}=\{E_{x}\}_{x\in X}
.

Proof.

The first part of the assertion is straightforward. Let
E
be any
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence from
A
to
B
satisfying (
3.1
).
Recall that the fibres of the corresponding bundles
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are given by
A_{x}:=A/I_{x}A
and
B_{x}:=B/I_{x}B
where
I_{x}:=\{f\in\mathrm{C_{0}}(X):f(x)=0\}
,
x\in X
.
By (
3.1
) for each
x\in X
, we have
(I_{x}A)E\subseteq E(I_{x}B)
.
Thus the quotient
E_{x}:=E/(EI_{x}B)
is naturally a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence from
A_{x}=A/I_{x}
to
B_{x}=B/I_{x}
, cf.
[
32
, Lemma 2.3]
(note that
E_{x}
is canonically isomorphic to the
A_{x}
-
B_{x}
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence
E\otimes_{B}B_{x}
considered in
[
46
,
23
]
).
Denoting by
\xi(x)
the image of
\xi
in the quotient
E_{x}=E/(EI_{x}B)
, we get
\|\xi(x)\|^{2}=\|\langle\xi(x),\xi(x)\rangle_{B_{x}}\|=\|\langle\xi,\xi\rangle(x)\|
, where
\langle\xi,\xi\rangle
is in the
\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
-algebra
B
.
Hence the map
X\ni x\mapsto\|\xi(x)\|
is upper semicontinuous, vanishes at infinity and its maximum is
\|\xi\|
.
Thus by Fell’s reconstruction theorem, there is a unique topology on
\mathcal{E}:=\{E_{x}\}_{x\in X}
such that sections
X\ni x\mapsto\xi(x)\in E_{x}
for
\xi\in E
are continuous.
The proof of
[
70
, Proposition C.24]
can be readily adapted to show that the image of
E
in
\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{E})
is dense.
Since the embedding
E\hookrightarrow\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{E})
is isometric, and
E
is complete, we conclude it must be an isomorphism.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

For any
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence bundle
\mathcal{E}=\{E_{x}\}_{x\in X}
from
\mathcal{A}=\{A_{x}\}_{x\in X}
to
\mathcal{B}=\{B_{x}\}_{x\in X}
the space
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{E})
is naturally a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence from
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{A})
to
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{B})
, and so
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{E})
is also a
\mathrm{C_{b}}(X)
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence.
Moreover,
\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{E})
can also be treated as a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence from
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{A})
to
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{B})
, a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-subcorrespondence of
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{E})
.

Example 3.4
.

Every
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-bundle
\mathcal{A}=\{A_{x}\}_{x\in X}
can be viewed as a (trivial)
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence bundle over
\mathcal{A}
, by viewing each fibre
A_{x}
as the trivial
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence.
Proposition
3.2
applied to such
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence bundles recovers the correspondence between
\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
-C
∗
-algebras and
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-bundles, extending the arguments used to establish the latter fact.

Example 3.5
.

If
\mathcal{A}=\{\mathbb{C}\}_{x\in X}
is the trivial one-dimensional
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-bundle, so that
\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A})=\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
, then a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence bundle from
\mathcal{A}
to a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-bundle
\mathcal{B}=\{B_{x}\}_{x\in X}
(with the associated
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra
B=\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{B})
), is simply a
Hilbert module bundle
over
\mathcal{B}=\{B_{x}\}_{x\in X}
,
see
[
40
, 1.7]
, because we may ignore the left action of
\mathcal{A}
(it carries no new information).
In view of Proposition
3.2
such Hilbert module bundles correspond to
\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
-Hilbert modules,
where by a
\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
-Hilbert module
over a
\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
-
\mathrm{\mathrm{C}^{*}}
-algebra
B
we mean a right Hilbert module
E
over
B
which is also
a
\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
-module such that

f\cdot\xi\cdot b=\xi\cdot(fb)
,
f\in\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
,
b\in B
,
\xi\in E
.
If, in addition,
\mathcal{B}=\{\mathbb{C}\}_{x\in X}
is a trivial bundle, then all Hilbert modules over
B=\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
are
\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
-Hilbert modules and they correspond to
continuous bundles of Hilbert spaces

\mathcal{H}=\{H_{x}\}_{x\in X}
, see
[
31
, Definition II.13.5]
or
[
59
]
.

Example 3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{A}=\{A_{x}\}_{x\in X}
be a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-bundle and let
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
The
\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra picture yields immediately that
\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathcal{A})=\{\mathbb{M}_{n}(A_{x})\}_{x\in X}
is again a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-bundle; and if
A
is the
\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra associated to
\mathcal{A}
, then
\mathbb{M}_{n}(A)
is the one associated to
\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathcal{A})
.
Next, if
\mathcal{E}=\{E_{x}\}_{x\in X}
is a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence bundle from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
as in Definition
3.1
, then
\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathcal{E})=\{\mathbb{M}_{n}(E_{x})\}_{x\in X}
, with
\mathbb{M}_{n}(E_{x})
constructed as in Example
1.11
and the natural topology on
\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathcal{E})
, becomes a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence bundle from
\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathcal{A})
to
\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathcal{B})
.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
\mathcal{E}=\{E_{x}\}_{x\in X}
be a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence bundle from
\mathcal{A}=\{A_{x}\}_{x\in X}
to
\mathcal{B}=\{B_{x}\}_{x\in X}
. We say that a section
\xi
of the bundle
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{E}):=\{\mathcal{M}(E_{x})\}_{x\in X}
of multiplier
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondences is
strictly continuous
if
a\cdot\xi,\xi\cdot b\in\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{E})
for any
a\in\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A})
,
b\in\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{B})
.
We denote by
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{E}))
the set of all bounded strictly continuous sections of
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{E})
.

For a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-bundle
\mathcal{A}=\{A_{x}\}_{x\in X}
, treated as a trivial correspondence bundle from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{A}
, the above definition of the multiplier bundle
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A})=\{\mathcal{M}(A_{x})\}_{x\in X}
is consistent with the one given in
[
1
, Section 3]
.
In particular, the following proposition generalises
[
1
, Theorem 3.3]
from
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-bundles to
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence bundles.

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
\mathcal{E}=\{E_{x}\}_{x\in X}
be a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence bundle from
\mathcal{A}=\{A_{x}\}_{x\in X}
to
\mathcal{B}=\{B_{x}\}_{x\in X}
.
Equipped with pointwise operations, the section algebras
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A}))
,
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{B}))
are
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebras and
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{E}))
is a
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A}))
-
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{B}))
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence isomorphic to the
\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A}))
-
\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{B}))
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence
\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{E}))
, where the isomorphism

\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{E}))\cong\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{E}))

is the unique morphism that extends the identities on
\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{E})
,
\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{B})
.
In particular,
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A}))\cong\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A}))
and
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{B}))\cong\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{B}))
as
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebras.

Proof.

It is immediate that bounded strictly continuous sections of
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{E})
form a Banach
\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A})
-
\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{B})
-bimodule with pointwise operations and norm
\|\xi\|:=\sup\|\xi(x)\|
.
When this construction is applied to the
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-bundle
\mathcal{A}
, we also see that
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A}))
is closed under pointwise multiplication, involution, and contains the unit section. So
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A}))
is naturally a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra that contains
\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A})
as a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-subalgebra, in fact, an ideal. We have a unital
*
-homomorphism
\pi_{A}:\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A}))\to\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A}))
where
[\pi_{A}(b)a](x)=b(x)\cdot a(x)
,
b\in\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A}))
,
a\in\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A})
,
x\in X
.
It is isometric and extends the identity on
\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A})
.
To show it is surjective, let
x\in X
and note that the evaluation map
\pi_{A,x}:\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A})\to A_{x}
,
\pi_{A,x}(a)=a(x)
, extends (uniquely) to a
*
-homomorphism
\overline{\pi}_{A,x}:\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A}))\to\mathcal{M}(A_{x})
. For
a\in\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A}))
the formula
\widehat{a}(x)=\overline{\pi}_{A,x}(a)
defines
\widehat{a}\in\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A}))
with
\pi_{A}(\widehat{a})=a
.
Hence we have
*
-isomorphisms

\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A}))\cong\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A})),\qquad\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{B}))\cong\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{B}))

which are in fact given by unique homomorphisms extending the identities on
\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{B})
, respectively.
A similar argument works for the
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A}))
-
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{B}))
-bimodule
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{E}))
.
Namely, the map
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{E}))\ni\xi\mapsto(R_{\xi},L_{\xi})\in\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{E}))
where
R_{\xi}(a):=a\xi
and
L_{\xi}(b):=\xi b
, is a linear isometry
\pi:\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{E}))\to\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{E}))
that extends the identity on
\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{E})
.
To see that
\pi
is surjective, note that, by Lemma
1.6
, for any
x\in X
, the map
\pi_{x}:\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{E})\to E_{x}
,
\pi_{x}(\xi)=\xi(x)
, extends (uniquely) to a linear map
\overline{\pi}_{x}:\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{E}))\to\mathcal{M}(E_{x})
such that
\overline{\pi}_{x}(a\xi b)=\overline{\pi}_{A,x}(a)(x)\overline{\pi}_{x}(\xi)\overline{\pi}_{B,x}
for
a\in\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A}))
,
b\in\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{B}))
.
For
\xi\in M(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{E}))
the formula
\widehat{\xi}(x)=\overline{\pi}_{x}(\xi)
defines
\widehat{\xi}\in\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A}))
with
\pi(\widehat{\xi}):=\xi
.
Hence
(\pi,\pi_{A},\pi_{B})
is an isometric isomorphism from the
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A}))
-
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{B}))
-bimodule
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{E}))
to the
\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A}))
-
\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{B}))
-bimodule
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{E})
.
It is the unique morphism that extends the identity morphism on
\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{E})
.
Its inverse maps the inner product in
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{E})
to the inner product in
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{E}))
defined pointwise.
∎

For a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-bundle
\mathcal{A}=\{A_{x}\}_{x\in X}
, the canonical identification
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A}))\cong\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A}))
from Proposition
3.8

allows us to regard
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A}))
as the multiplier algebra of
\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A})
. In particular, this justifies writing informally
\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A}))=\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A}))
in the sequel.

Remark 3.9
.

For a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-bundle
\mathcal{A}=\{A_{x}\}_{x\in X}
, the multiplier bundle
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A})=\{\mathcal{M}(A_{x})\}_{x\in X}
is usually not a Banach bundle with the ‘strict topology’.
It may also happen that
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A}))\neq\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{A})
even when
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{A}
as sets.
For instance, let
\mathcal{A}
be the bundle corresponding to the
C([0,1])
-algebra:

A=\left\{a\in C([0,1],\mathbb{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})):a(0)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\lambda&0\\
0&0\end{array}\right),\ \lambda\in\mathbb{C}\right\}.

Then
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A})
because all the fibres
A_{x}
,
x\in[0,1]
are unital (
A_{0}\simeq\mathbb{C}
,
A_{x}\simeq\mathbb{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
for
x>0
).
But
A=\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A})=\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{A})
is not unital and so it is smaller than
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A}))=\mathcal{M}(A)
. In fact we have,

\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A}))=\mathcal{M}(A)=\left\{a\in C([0,1],\mathbb{M}_{2}(\mathbb{C})):a(0)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\lambda&0\\
0&\mu\end{array}\right),\ \lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{C}\right\}.

In general, the strictly continuous sections of
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A})
coincide with continuous sections of
\mathcal{A}
if and only if the bundle
\mathcal{A}
has a
continuous unital section
, i.e. each fibre
A_{x}
,
x\in X
is unital and the unit section
X\ni x\mapsto 1_{x}\in A_{x}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
is continuous.
This latter condition was made a standing assumption in
[
42
]
.

The tensor product of
\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondences is a
\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence, cf.
[
46
, Proposition 4.1]
.
In terms of bundles this corresponds to the following construction.

Example 3.10

(Tensor product of
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence bundles)
.

If
\mathcal{E}=\{E_{x}\}_{x\in X}
and
\mathcal{F}=\{F_{x}\}_{x\in X}
are
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence bundles from
\mathcal{A}=\{A_{x}\}_{x\in X}
to
\mathcal{B}=\{B_{x}\}_{x\in X}
and from
\mathcal{B}=\{B_{x}\}_{x\in X}
to
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{x}\}_{x\in X}
respectively, then the collection of
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence tensor products
\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{F}:=\{E_{x}\otimes_{B_{x}}F_{x}\}_{x\in X}
is naturally a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence bundle from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{C}
.
Indeed, for every
\xi_{i}\in\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{E})
,
\zeta_{i}\in\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{F})
,
i=1,\ldots,n
, consider the section
x\mapsto(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}\otimes\zeta_{i})(x):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}(x)\otimes\zeta_{i}(x)\in\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{F}
.
It follows from Definition
3.1
that the map
x\mapsto\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}\otimes\zeta_{i}(x)\|^{2}=\|\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\langle\zeta_{i}(x),\langle\xi_{i}(x),\xi_{j}(x)\rangle_{B_{x}}\zeta_{j}(x)\rangle_{C_{x}}\|
is upper semicontinuous.
Hence
\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{F}
can be equipped with a unique topology such that the sections
\xi\otimes\zeta
,
\xi\in\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{E})
,
\zeta\in\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{F})
, are continuous.
Then
\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{F}
is a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence bundle from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{C}
and we have a canonical isomorphism

\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{F})\cong\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{E})\otimes_{\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{B})}\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{F}).

of
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondences from
\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A})
to
\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{C})
.
Combining Lemma
1.12
and Proposition
3.8
we also have the embedding
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{E}))\otimes_{\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{B}))}\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{F}))\rightarrowtail\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}\mathcal{F}))
of
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondences from
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A}))\cong\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A}))
to
\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{C}))\cong\mathcal{M}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{C}))
.

Example 3.11

(Direct sums of
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence bundles)
.

Let
\mathcal{E}=\{E_{x}\}_{x\in X}
and
\mathcal{F}=\{F_{x}\}_{x\in X}
be
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-bundles from
\mathcal{A}=\{A_{x}\}_{x\in X}
to
\mathcal{B}=\{B_{x}\}_{x\in X}
.
We equip the family
\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{F}:=\{E_{x}\oplus F_{x}\}_{x\in X}
of direct sums of
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence bundles with the unique topology such that for all
\xi\in\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{E})
,
\zeta\in\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{F})
the section
x\mapsto\xi\oplus\zeta(x):=\xi(x)\oplus\zeta(x)
is in
\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{F})
.
This is a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence bundle from
\mathcal{A}=\{A_{x}\}_{x\in X}
to
\mathcal{B}=\{B_{x}\}_{x\in X}
and we have a canonical isomorphism of
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondences

\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{F})\cong\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{E})\oplus\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{F}),\qquad\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{F})\cong\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{E})\oplus\mathrm{C_{b}}(\mathcal{F}).

More generally, if
\{\mathcal{E}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is a family of
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence bundles from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
, with
\mathcal{E}_{i}=\{E_{x}^{i}\}_{x\in X}
for each
i\in I
, then we define the direct sum
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence bundle by putting
\oplus_{i\in I}\mathcal{E}_{i}:=\{\oplus_{i\in I}E_{x}^{i}\}_{x\in X}
and equipping it with the unique topology such that for sections
\xi_{i}\in\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{E}_{i})
,
i\in I
, such that
\sum_{i\in I}\|\xi_{i}(x)\|^{2}<\infty
for every
x\in X
, the section
X\ni x\to\sum_{i\in I}\xi_{i}(x)\in\oplus_{i\in I}\mathcal{E}_{i}
is continuous.

Finally we comment on morphisms between
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence bundles.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
\mathcal{E}=\{E_{x}\}_{x\in X}
and
\mathcal{F}=\{F_{x}\}_{x\in X}
be Banach bundles.
The relation

L(\xi)(x)=L_{x}(\xi(x)),\qquad x\in X,\ \xi\in\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{E}),

establishes a bijective correspondence between bounded
\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
-linear maps
L:\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{E})\to\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{F})
and bundles
\{L_{x}\}_{x\in X}
of linear operators
L_{x}:E_{x}\to F_{x}
, such that
\sup_{x\in X}\|L_{x}\|<\infty
and the map
\mathcal{E}\supseteq E_{x}\ni\xi\longmapsto L_{x}(\xi)\in F_{x}\subseteq\mathcal{F}
is continuous.

Proof.

This is standard, see for instance (the proofs of)
[
44
, Propositions 3.2, 3.5]
.
∎

4.
Fell bundles and twisted actions

In this section we introduce twisted actions of groupoids on
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-bundles and describe the associated crossed products. From now on, throughout this paper,

\mathcal{G}
is a
locally compact Hausdorff étale groupoid
with the
unit space

X:=\mathcal{G}^{(0)}
.

We denote by
r,s:\mathcal{G}\to X\subseteq\mathcal{G}
the
range
and
source
maps and by
\mathcal{G}^{(2)}=\{(g,h)\in\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{G}:s(g)=r(h)\}
the set of
composable pairs
.
Similarly we define
composable triples

\mathcal{G}^{(3)}
, and so on.
We refer to
[
56
,
29
,
65
]
for more information on étale groupoids.
In this section we will define their (twisted) actions on
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-bundles.
We will however begin the discussion in the context of general Fell bundles.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
Fell bundle over the groupoid
\mathcal{G}
, see
[
15
, Section 2]
or
[
40
,
67
]
.
For the convenience of the reader we recall some basic facts here.
By definition,
\mathcal{A}
is an upper-semicontinuous Banach bundle
\mathcal{A}=\{A_{g}\}_{g\in\mathcal{G}}
equipped with a continuous multiplication
{\cdot}:\{(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}:a\in A_{g},\ b\in A_{h},\ (g,h)\in\mathcal{G}^{(2)}\}\to\mathcal{A}
and a continuous involution

{}^{*}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
such that for all
g,h\in\mathcal{G}
with
(g,h)\in\mathcal{G}^{(2)}

A_{g}\times A_{h}\ni(a,b)\mapsto ab\in A_{gh},\qquad\mathcal{A}\ni A_{g}\ni a\mapsto a^{*}\in A_{g^{-1}},

satisfying the standard set of axioms (note that we write simply
ab
in lieu of
a\cdot b
).
The linear space
\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{A})
is naturally a
*
-algebra with algebraic operations coming from the Fell bundle: for
a,b\in\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{A})
we put

(a*b)(g):=\sum_{h\in\mathcal{G}_{s(g)}}a(gh^{-1})b(h)=\sum_{h\in\mathcal{G}^{r(g)}}a(h)b(h^{-1}g)\quad\text{and}\quad a^{*}(g):=a(g^{-1})^{*}.

Here, for
x\in X
, we put
\mathcal{G}_{x}:=\{g\in\mathcal{G}:s(g)=x\}
and
\mathcal{G}^{x}:=\{g\in\mathcal{G}:r(g)=x\}
.

Definition 4.1
.

The
full section
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra

\mathrm{C}^{*}(\mathcal{A})
of the Fell bundle
\mathcal{A}
is the completion of
\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{A})
in the maximal
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-norm.

One shows that the largest
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-(semi)norm indeed exists on
\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{A})
, see
[
14
]
.
To prove that it is a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-norm, one constructs the reduced norm as follows.
The restriction
\mathcal{A}|_{X}=\{A_{x}\}_{x\in X}
of
\mathcal{A}
to
X
is a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-bundle and
\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{A}|_{X})\subseteq\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{A})
is a
*
-subalgebra.
The inclusion
\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{A}|_{X})\subseteq\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{A})
extends to a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-inclusion
\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A}|_{X})\subseteq\mathrm{C}^{*}(\mathcal{A})
.
The restriction map
a\mapsto a|_{X}
,
a\in\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{A})
, extends to a conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}:\mathrm{C}^{*}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A}|_{X})
.
Consider the Hilbert
A_{x}
-module direct sum

\ell^{2}(\mathcal{A})_{x}:=\Big\{\xi:\mathcal{G}_{x}\to\mathcal{A}:\xi(g)\in A_{g},\,g\in\mathcal{G}_{x},\;\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}}\xi(g)^{*}\xi(g)\text{ converges in $A_{x}$}\Big\}=\bigoplus_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}}A_{g}.

Then we have a representation
\Lambda_{x}:\mathrm{C}^{*}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{L}(\ell^{2}(\mathcal{A})_{x})
determined by the formula

[\Lambda_{x}(a)\xi](g)=\sum_{h\in\mathcal{G}_{x}}a(gh^{-1})\xi(h),\qquad a\in\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{A}),\ \xi\in\ell^{2}(A_{x}),\ g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}.

There is a unique topology on
\ell^{2}(\mathcal{A})=\{\ell^{2}(\mathcal{A})_{x}\}_{x\in X}
making it a Hilbert module bundle over
\mathcal{A}|_{X}
, and such that
\mathrm{C_{0}}(\ell^{2}(\mathcal{A}))
contains
x\mapsto\bigoplus_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}}a(g)
, for all
a\in\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{A})
, cf.
[
40
, 3.3]
.
The field of representations
\{\Lambda_{x}\}_{x\in X}
induces a representation
\Lambda:\mathrm{C}^{*}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\ell^{2}(\mathcal{A})))

\big(\Lambda(a)\xi\big)(x)=\Lambda_{x}(a)\xi(x),\qquad a\in\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{A}),\ \xi\in\mathrm{C_{0}}(\ell^{2}(\mathcal{A})),\ x\in X,

that we call the
regular representation
of
\mathrm{C}^{*}(\mathcal{A})
.
This representation is faithful on
\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{A})
, but not on
\mathrm{C}^{*}(\mathcal{A})
, in general.
When
\Lambda
is faithful we say that
\mathcal{A}
has the
weak containment property
.
This is implied by
amenability
of
\mathcal{G}

[
64
]
or some sort of amenability of
\mathcal{A}

[
39
,
16
]
, but there are examples of non-amenable groupoids with the weak containment property
[
69
]
.

Notice that
\mathrm{C_{0}}(\ell^{2}(\mathcal{A}))
is a
\mathrm{C}^{*}(\mathcal{A})
-
\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A}|_{X})
-
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence that we call the
regular
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-correspondence
for
\mathcal{A}
.
It can be defined equivalently as the completion of the pre-Hilbert module
\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{A})
with respect to the inner product
\langle a,b\rangle_{\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A}|_{X})}:=\mathbb{E}(a^{*}b)
.
Then
\Lambda:\mathrm{C}^{*}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{L}\left(\mathrm{C_{0}}(\ell^{2}(\mathcal{A}))\right)
is determined by the multiplication in
\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{A})
:
\Lambda(a)b=a*b
for
a\in\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathrm{C}^{*}(\mathcal{A})
and
b\in\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathrm{C_{0}}(\ell^{2}(\mathcal{A}))
.

Definition 4.2
.

The
reduced section
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra

\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\red}(\mathcal{A})
is the completion of
\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{A})
in the norm
\|a\|_{\red}:=\sup_{x\in X}\|\Lambda_{x}(a)\|
.
Thus
\mathrm{C}^{*}_{\red}(\mathcal{A})\cong\Lambda(\mathrm{C}^{*}(\mathcal{A}))
and the restriction map
\mathbb{E}:\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathrm{C_{c}}(\mathcal{A}|_{X})
extends to a faithful conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}:\mathrm{C}^{*}_{r}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A}|_{X})
.

We briefly comment on the relationship between Fell bundles over the groupoid
\mathcal{G}
and Fell bundles over inverse semigroups.
This will be useful when studying representations of the section
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebras and also checking that twisted groupoid actions yield Fell bundles.
To this end recall that the family of (open) bisections of the groupoid
\mathcal{G}
, i.e.

\Bis(\mathcal{G})=\{U\subseteq\mathcal{G}:\text{$U$ is open and $r|_{U},s|_{U}$ are injective}\}.

with operations induced by composition and inverse from
\mathcal{G}
forms naturally a unital inverse semigroup.
The unit space
X
is the unit for the inverse semigroup
\Bis(\mathcal{G})
.

Let now
S
be any unital inverse semigroup with unit
1
.
A
Fell bundle over the inverse semigroup

S
is a family
\{A_{t}\}_{t\in S}
of Hilbert
A
-
A
-bimodules, where
A:=A_{1}
is a
\mathrm{C}^{*}
-algebra, and hence also a trivial Hilbert
A
-bimodule, equipped with a multiplication given by bimodule embeddings
A_{t}\otimes_{A}A_{u}\to A_{tu}
,
t,u\in S
, which are associative in an appropriate sense and induce isomorphisms
A_{t}\otimes_{A}A_{t^{*}}\otimes_{A}A_{t}\cong A_{t}
,
t\in S
.
See
[
16
]
for details and relationship with previous equivalent definitions.
In particular, this data induces inclusion maps
j_{t,u}:A_{t}\to A_{u}
and involutions
*:A_{t}\to A_{t^{*}}
compatible with the above structures.

Example 4.3

(Inverse semigroup Fell bundle from a groupoid Fell bundle)
.

Every Fell bundle
\mathcal{A}=\{A_{g}\}_{g\in\mathcal{G}}
over
\mathcal{G}

induces
a Fell bundle
\mathcal{A}_{\sem}=\{A_{U}\}_{U\in\Bis(\mathcal{G})}
over
\Bis(\mathcal{G})
, where the fibres are the spaces
A_{U}:=\mathrm{C_{0}}(\mathcal{A}|_{U})
,
U\in\Bis(\mathcal{G})
, and multiplication maps
A_{U}\times A_{V}\to A_{UV}
and involution maps
A_{U}\to A_{U^{*}}
are given by multiplication and involution on
\mathcal{A}
.
We have natural inclusions
A_{U}\subseteq A_{V}
for
U\subseteq V
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{A}
is saturated, that is
\overline{A_{g}\cdot A_{h}}=A_{gh}

for all
(g,h)\in\mathcal{G}^{(2)}
, if and only if
\mathcal{A}_{\sem}
is saturated, that is
\overline{A_{U}\cdot A_{V}}=A_{UV}
for all
U,V\in\Bis(\mathcal{G})
.

In the saturated case we have the following elegant characterisation of inverse semigroup Fell bundles coming from groupoid Fell bundles as described in Example
4.3
:

Theorem 4.4

(
[
18
, Theorem 6.1]
)
.

Let
S:=\Bis(\mathcal{G})
be the inverse semigroup of bisections of
\mathcal{G}
.
A saturated Fell bundle
\mathcal{A}_{\sem}=\{A_{U}\}_{U\in S}
over
S
is induced from a Fell bundle
\mathcal{A}=\{A_{g}\}_{g\in\mathcal{G}}
over
\mathcal{G}
, as described in Example
4.3
, if and only if the map
X\supseteq U\mapsto A_{U}\triangleleft A_{X}
from the lattice of open sets in
X
to the lattice of ideals in
A_{X}
preserves arbitrary suprema.

If this is the case, the Fell bundle
\mathcal{A}
over
\mathcal{G}
is unique up to an isomorphism.

Remark 4.5
.

For further reference we describe how the bundle
\mathcal{A}
over
\mathcal{G}
in Theorem
4.4
is constructed from
\mathcal{A}_{\sem}
in Theorem
4.4
.
If the map
X\supseteq U\mapsto A_{U}\triangleleft A_{X}
preserves suprema, then (since it automatically preserves infima) by general Stone duality
A_{X}
is a
\mathrm{C_{0}}(X)
-algebra where
A_{U}=A_{X}\mathrm{C_{0}}(U)
for every open
U\subseteq X
.
In particular,
A_{X}\cong\mathrm{C_{0}}(\{A_{x}\}_{x\in X})
where
A_{x}:=A_{X}/I_{x}
and
I_{x}:=A_{X}\mathrm{C_{0}}(X\setminus\{x\})
for
x\in X
.
To extend this construction to
A_{U}
for
U\in S
, we use that
A_{U}
is naturally a Morita equivalence
A_{r(U)}
-
A_{s(U)}
-bimodule.
Namely, for any
g\in U
we have
A_{U}I_{s(g)}=I_{r(g)}A_{U}=I_{r(g)}A_{U}I_{s(g)}
and this subspace of
A_{U}
denoted by
I_{U,g}
is naturally an
I_{r(g)}
-
I_{s(g)}
-Hilbert bimodule.
Hence the quotient
A_{U,g}:=A_{U}/I_{U,g}
is naturally an
A_{r(g)}
-
A_{s(g)}
-Hilbert bimodule.
Writing
a_{U}(g)
for the image of
a\in A_{U}
in
A_{U,g}
we have a unique topology on the bundle
\{A_{U,g}\}_{g\in U}
such that we have an isomorphism

A_{U}\cong\mathrm{C_{0}}(\{A_{U,g}\}_{g\in U})\quad\text{ where }\quad A_{U}\ni a\longmapsto a_{U}\in\mathrm{C_{0}}(\{A_{U,g}\}_{g\in U}).

Using the inclusion maps from
\mathcal{A}_{\sem}
one may piece together the above structures to obtain a Banach bundle
\mathcal{A}=\{A_{g}\}_{g\in\mathcal{G}}
where
A_{g}\cong A_{U,g}
canonically for any
U\in\Bis(\mathcal{G})
containing
g\in\mathcal{G}
.
Then the formulas

a_{U}(g)^{*}:=a^{*}_{U^{*}}(g^{-1}),\qquad a_{U}(g)\cdot b_{V}(h):=(a\cdot b)_{UV}(gh),\qquad\text{ for }a\in A_{U},b\in A_{V},

induce the desired Fell bundle structure on
\mathcal{A}
.

Twisted actions
